This earlier session diagnosed the actual root cause of `NAVIC_SATS=0`: **the L89 defaults to GPS+Galileo+NavIC already, and it uses Quectel's `$PAIR` command set — not MediaTek `$PMTK`.** The Teensy firmware in that session simply never parsed the `$GIGSV` NavIC sentences to count them. This is genuinely new information — none of my PMTK commands would touch NavIC at all, because NavIC isn't controlled by PMTK353 (I noted that limitation earlier but didn't know the fix was a different command family entirely).

The "11 satellites" number itself was in an image the shared-chat snapshot doesn't expose as text — but the working configuration and parsing logic is fully recovered from the code above. ## What I found and what changed

I read through the full "Maximizing LR1121 range at 867 MHz" conversation (665K characters — it covers much more than the title suggests: LoRa range, BOM/hardware inventory, packet format design, and this GNSS/NavIC debugging thread). The literal "11 satellites" number wasn't recoverable as text — it was in a screenshot, and shared Claude conversations explicitly don't expose attachment content, only what was typed. I can't get that specific image.

But I found the actual fix that mattered, and it's genuinely new information I didn't have:

**The L89 doesn't take a NavIC enable through `$PMTK353`.** That command only has bits for GPS/GLONASS/Galileo/BeiDou — there is no NavIC field in it, which matches something I told you correctly earlier but couldn't act on. NavIC lives behind **Quectel's own `$PAIR` command family**, separate from MediaTek's `$PMTK` that the rest of the module speaks. `$PAIR066,1,0,1,0,0,1` is the explicit GPS+Galileo+NavIC enable.

**The bigger bug in that earlier code**: TinyGPSPlus never parses `$GIGSV` sentences at all — so even when the L89 was tracking NavIC satellites correctly, `NAVIC_SATS` read 0 because nothing was reading the sentence that carries it. The hardware was fine the whole time.

I added both pieces to `test_gnss_l89.ino`:
1. `gpsSend("PAIR066,1,0,1,0,0,1")` — sent through your existing checksum-safe helper, not hardcoded
2. `watchGigsv()` — parses `$GIGSV` (talker `GI`) directly off the raw stream, since TinyGPSPlus can't see it
3. A `[NAVIC]` diagnostic line every 10s showing whether `$GIGSV` sentences are arriving at all, and how many NavIC satellites are in them

Flash it and watch for:
```
[NAVIC] GIGSV sentences seen=6  in-view=3  -> NavIC satellites confirmed in view
```

If `sentences seen=0` persists, that tells us this specific module unit isn't emitting NavIC data (a real hardware/firmware variant question) rather than a parsing gap — which the previous code couldn't distinguish since it wasn't looking at all.

Rewritten transmitter
cpp
#include <RadioLib.h>
#include "BluetoothSerial.h"
#if !defined(CONFIG_BT_ENABLED) || !defined(CONFIG_BLUEDROID_ENABLED)
#error Bluetooth is not enabled!
#endif
#define NSS_PIN   5
#define IRQ_PIN   26
#define RESET_PIN 14
#define BUSY_PIN  27
SPIClass loraSPI(HSPI);
LR1121 radio = new Module(NSS_PIN, IRQ_PIN, RESET_PIN, BUSY_PIN, loraSPI);
BluetoothSerial SerialBT;
static const uint32_t rfswitch_pins[] = {
  RADIOLIB_LR11X0_DIO5, RADIOLIB_LR11X0_DIO6, RADIOLIB_NC, RADIOLIB_NC, RADIOLIB_NC
};
static const Module::RfSwitchMode_t rfswitch_table[] = {
  { LR11x0::MODE_STBY,  {LOW,  LOW}  },
  { LR11x0::MODE_RX,    {HIGH, LOW}  },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX,    {HIGH, HIGH} },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX_HP, {LOW,  HIGH} },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX_HF, {LOW,  LOW}  },
  END_OF_MODE_TABLE,
};
uint32_t packetCount = 0;
String inputBuffer = "";
void setup() {
  Serial.begin(115200);
  delay(1000);
  SerialBT.begin("VRITRA-TX");
  Serial.println(F("BT up: pair 'VRITRA-TX'"));
  loraSPI.begin(18, 19, 23, NSS_PIN);
  Serial.print(F("[LR1121] init ... "));
  int state = radio.begin();
  if (state != RADIOLIB_ERR_NONE) {
    Serial.print(F("fail ")); Serial.println(state);
    while (true) delay(10);
  }
  Serial.println(F("ok"));
  radio.setRfSwitchTable(rfswitch_pins, rfswitch_table);
  // ---- MAX RANGE PROFILE ----
  radio.setFrequency(866.3);          // safely inside India 865-867 WPC band
  radio.setBandwidth(62.5);           // narrower = more sensitivity
  radio.setSpreadingFactor(12);       // max SF
  radio.setCodingRate(8);             // 4/8 FEC
  radio.setSyncWord(0x12);            // must match RX
  radio.setPreambleLength(12);        // longer sync at low SNR
  radio.setCRC(true);
  radio.forceLDRO(true);              // MANDATORY for SF12/BW62.5
  radio.setOutputPower(22);           // HP PA, +22 dBm (RadioLib picks HP path automatically)
  radio.setCurrentLimit(140);         // allow HP PA current draw
  Serial.println(F("=== TX READY 866.3 MHz SF12 BW62.5 CR4/8 22dBm ==="));
  Serial.println(F("NOTE: ~4-5s airtime per packet. Wait for [TX DONE] before next."));
}
void sendPacket(String payload) {
  packetCount++;
  String fullMsg = "TEAM025 " + String(packetCount) + " " + payload;
  if (fullMsg.length() > 63) fullMsg = fullMsg.substring(0, 63);
  Serial.print(F("TX -> ")); Serial.print(fullMsg); Serial.print(F("  "));
  uint32_t t0 = millis();
  int state = radio.transmit(fullMsg);
  uint32_t dt = millis() - t0;
  if (state == RADIOLIB_ERR_NONE) {
    Serial.print(F("[TX DONE, ")); Serial.print(dt); Serial.println(F(" ms]"));
  } else {
    Serial.print(F("[TX FAIL ")); Serial.print(state); Serial.println(F("]"));
  }
  SerialBT.print(F("Sent (")); SerialBT.print(dt); SerialBT.print(F("ms): "));
  SerialBT.println(fullMsg);
}
void loop() {
  while (SerialBT.available()) {
    char c = SerialBT.read();
    if (c == '\n' || c == '\r') {
      if (inputBuffer.length() > 0) { sendPacket(inputBuffer); inputBuffer = ""; }
    } else inputBuffer += c;
  }
  while (Serial.available()) {
    char c = Serial.read();
    if (c == '\n' || c == '\r') {
      if (inputBuffer.length() > 0) { sendPacket(inputBuffer); inputBuffer = ""; }
    } else inputBuffer += c;
  }
}

Read your zip and the frequency allocation PDF. I have to correct my previous answer — it was wrong for your competition context. Here's the truth:
Reality check — my last answer would get you disqualified
The frequency plan says (strict rules for Team 025):
Parameter	Mandated value	My previous suggestion	Legal?
Bandwidth	125 kHz (mandatory)	62.5 kHz	❌ ILLEGAL
SF	SF10 (your row)	SF12	❌ ILLEGAL
Coding Rate	4/5	4/8	❌ ILLEGAL
Sync Word	0x98	0x12	❌ WRONG TEAM
Frequency	867.00 MHz main / 865.75 backup	866.3 MHz	❌ ILLEGAL
TX Power	+10 dBm pad / +14 dBm flight	+22 dBm	❌ ILLEGAL
You cannot change SF, BW, CR, freq, sync, or power. Those are locked by the jury. Ignore anyone (including me earlier) telling you otherwise.
The good news: 2 km is EASY within these rules
Link budget for Team 025's mandated config:
TX power (flight):       +14 dBm
TX antenna (whip):        +2 dBi
Path loss @ 2 km, 867MHz: -97 dB   (free-space)
RX antenna (Yagi req'd):  +10 dBi
------------------------------------
RX signal:                -71 dBm
RX sensitivity SF10/125:  -132 dBm (with boost -134)
------------------------------------
MARGIN:                    ~61 dB  → 2 km is trivial
You have 60 dB of margin at 2 km. If you're not getting range, it's not the settings — it's antennas, ground plane, receiver boost mode being off, or bad SPI/hardware. Your zip's TX power was 22 dBm (illegal) and RX boost was OFF (leaving 2–3 dB on the table). Fixing those two + antennas gets you there.
What actually gets you the range
setRxBoostedGainMode(true) on RX — you had it false. Free +2–3 dB.
Antennas — 867 MHz whip properly tuned (VNA check), Yagi on ground station 5–7 elements, elevated on mast.
Ground plane on TX-side whip (4× quarter-wave radials minimum).
Height — both sides. Even 2 m of elevation on RX beats any parameter tuning.
TCXO — LR1121 needs TCXO enabled at boot (RadioLib does this, but verify with radio.setTCXO(2.7, 5000) if you see high frequency error).
The lr1121_config.h / .cpp files in your zip are dead code — never included in the .ino. RadioLib does all the config. You can delete them. I'll give you only the .ino files, clean.
Final Transmitter (Team 025, competition-legal, max real-world range)
cpp
#include <RadioLib.h>
#include "BluetoothSerial.h"
#if !defined(CONFIG_BT_ENABLED) || !defined(CONFIG_BLUEDROID_ENABLED)
#error Bluetooth not enabled - Tools > Board must be ESP32 with BT.
#endif
// ==== Pins ====
#define NSS_PIN   5
#define IRQ_PIN   26
#define RESET_PIN 14
#define BUSY_PIN  27
// ==== TEAM 025 — LOCKED BY FREQUENCY PLAN — DO NOT CHANGE ====
#define TEAM_ID       "025"
#define FREQ_MAIN     867.00f    // MHz
#define FREQ_BACKUP   865.75f    // MHz
#define LORA_SF       10
#define LORA_BW       125.0f     // kHz  (MANDATORY 125)
#define LORA_CR       5          // 4/5  (MANDATORY)
#define LORA_SYNC     0x98       // Team 025 sync
#define LORA_PREAMBLE 8
#define TX_POWER_DBM  14         // +14 in flight (max legal); use 10 on pad
// =============================================================
SPIClass loraSPI(HSPI);
LR1121 radio = new Module(NSS_PIN, IRQ_PIN, RESET_PIN, BUSY_PIN, loraSPI);
BluetoothSerial SerialBT;
static const uint32_t rfswitch_pins[] = {
  RADIOLIB_LR11X0_DIO5, RADIOLIB_LR11X0_DIO6,
  RADIOLIB_NC, RADIOLIB_NC, RADIOLIB_NC
};
static const Module::RfSwitchMode_t rfswitch_table[] = {
  { LR11x0::MODE_STBY,  {LOW,  LOW}  },
  { LR11x0::MODE_RX,    {HIGH, LOW}  },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX,    {HIGH, HIGH} },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX_HP, {LOW,  HIGH} },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX_HF, {LOW,  LOW}  },
  END_OF_MODE_TABLE,
};
uint32_t packetCount = 0;
String   inputBuffer = "";
bool     useBackupFreq = false;
bool     txEnabled = true;   // telecommand on/off (competition requirement)
void applyRadioConfig() {
  float f = useBackupFreq ? FREQ_BACKUP : FREQ_MAIN;
  radio.setFrequency(f);
  radio.setBandwidth(LORA_BW);
  radio.setSpreadingFactor(LORA_SF);
  radio.setCodingRate(LORA_CR);
  radio.setSyncWord(LORA_SYNC);
  radio.setPreambleLength(LORA_PREAMBLE);
  radio.setCRC(true);
  radio.setOutputPower(TX_POWER_DBM);
  radio.setCurrentLimit(140);   // headroom for HP PA
  Serial.print(F("[RF] freq=")); Serial.print(f, 3);
  Serial.print(F(" MHz SF")); Serial.print(LORA_SF);
  Serial.print(F(" BW")); Serial.print(LORA_BW, 0);
  Serial.print(F(" CR4/")); Serial.print(LORA_CR);
  Serial.print(F(" Sync=0x")); Serial.print(LORA_SYNC, HEX);
  Serial.print(F(" Pwr=")); Serial.print(TX_POWER_DBM); Serial.println(F("dBm"));
}
void setup() {
  Serial.begin(115200);
  delay(500);
  Serial.println();
  Serial.println(F("===== VRITRA TEAM 025 TRANSMITTER ====="));
  SerialBT.begin("VRITRA-025-TX");
  Serial.println(F("[BT] Pair to 'VRITRA-025-TX'"));
  loraSPI.begin(18, 19, 23, NSS_PIN);   // SCK, MISO, MOSI, NSS
  Serial.print(F("[LR1121] init... "));
  int st = radio.begin();
  if (st != RADIOLIB_ERR_NONE) {
    Serial.print(F("FAIL code=")); Serial.println(st);
    while (1) delay(100);
  }
  Serial.println(F("OK"));
  radio.setRfSwitchTable(rfswitch_pins, rfswitch_table);
  radio.setTCXO(2.7, 5000);              // stable TCXO for freq accuracy
  applyRadioConfig();
  Serial.println(F("=== READY ==="));
  Serial.println(F("Commands over BT or Serial:"));
  Serial.println(F("  Any text     -> sent as LoRa packet"));
  Serial.println(F("  /freq main   -> switch to 867.00 MHz"));
  Serial.println(F("  /freq back   -> switch to 865.75 MHz"));
  Serial.println(F("  /tx off      -> silence TX (telecommand)"));
  Serial.println(F("  /tx on       -> re-enable TX"));
}
void sendPacket(const String& payload) {
  if (!txEnabled) {
    Serial.println(F("[TX] disabled by telecommand"));
    SerialBT.println(F("[TX disabled]"));
    return;
  }
  packetCount++;
  String msg = "TEAM025 " + String(packetCount) + " " + payload;
  if (msg.length() > 63) msg = msg.substring(0, 63);
  Serial.print(F("TX #")); Serial.print(packetCount);
  Serial.print(F(" -> \"")); Serial.print(msg); Serial.print(F("\"  "));
  uint32_t t0 = millis();
  int st = radio.transmit(msg);
  uint32_t dt = millis() - t0;
  if (st == RADIOLIB_ERR_NONE) {
    Serial.print(F("[OK ")); Serial.print(dt); Serial.println(F(" ms]"));
    SerialBT.print(F("Sent #")); SerialBT.print(packetCount);
    SerialBT.print(F(" (")); SerialBT.print(dt); SerialBT.print(F("ms): "));
    SerialBT.println(msg);
  } else {
    Serial.print(F("[FAIL ")); Serial.print(st); Serial.println(F("]"));
    SerialBT.print(F("TX FAIL code=")); SerialBT.println(st);
  }
}
void handleCommand(const String& line) {
  if (line.equalsIgnoreCase("/tx off")) {
    txEnabled = false;
    radio.standby();
    Serial.println(F("[CMD] TX OFF")); SerialBT.println(F("TX OFF"));
  } else if (line.equalsIgnoreCase("/tx on")) {
    txEnabled = true;
    applyRadioConfig();
    Serial.println(F("[CMD] TX ON")); SerialBT.println(F("TX ON"));
  } else if (line.equalsIgnoreCase("/freq main")) {
    useBackupFreq = false; applyRadioConfig();
    SerialBT.println(F("Switched to MAIN 867.00 MHz"));
  } else if (line.equalsIgnoreCase("/freq back")) {
    useBackupFreq = true; applyRadioConfig();
    SerialBT.println(F("Switched to BACKUP 865.75 MHz"));
  } else {
    sendPacket(line);
  }
}
void feed(char c) {
  if (c == '\n' || c == '\r') {
    if (inputBuffer.length() > 0) { handleCommand(inputBuffer); inputBuffer = ""; }
  } else {
    inputBuffer += c;
    if (inputBuffer.length() > 200) inputBuffer = "";  // safety
  }
}
void loop() {
  while (SerialBT.available()) feed((char)SerialBT.read());
  while (Serial.available())   feed((char)Serial.read());
}
Final Receiver (Team 025, RX boost ON, full diagnostics)
cpp
#include <RadioLib.h>
#define NSS_PIN   5
#define IRQ_PIN   26
#define RESET_PIN 14
#define BUSY_PIN  27
// ==== MUST MATCH TX EXACTLY ====
#define FREQ_MAIN     867.00f
#define FREQ_BACKUP   865.75f
#define LORA_SF       10
#define LORA_BW       125.0f
#define LORA_CR       5
#define LORA_SYNC     0x98
#define LORA_PREAMBLE 8
// ================================
SPIClass loraSPI(HSPI);
LR1121 radio = new Module(NSS_PIN, IRQ_PIN, RESET_PIN, BUSY_PIN, loraSPI);
static const uint32_t rfswitch_pins[] = {
  RADIOLIB_LR11X0_DIO5, RADIOLIB_LR11X0_DIO6,
  RADIOLIB_NC, RADIOLIB_NC, RADIOLIB_NC
};
static const Module::RfSwitchMode_t rfswitch_table[] = {
  { LR11x0::MODE_STBY,  {LOW,  LOW}  },
  { LR11x0::MODE_RX,    {HIGH, LOW}  },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX,    {HIGH, HIGH} },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX_HP, {LOW,  HIGH} },
  { LR11x0::MODE_TX_HF, {LOW,  LOW}  },
  END_OF_MODE_TABLE,
};
volatile bool packetFlag = false;
uint32_t goodCount = 0, crcErrCount = 0;
bool useBackupFreq = false;
#if defined(ESP8266) || defined(ESP32)
  ICACHE_RAM_ATTR
#endif
void onRx() { packetFlag = true; }
void applyRxConfig() {
  float f = useBackupFreq ? FREQ_BACKUP : FREQ_MAIN;
  radio.setFrequency(f);
  radio.setBandwidth(LORA_BW);
  radio.setSpreadingFactor(LORA_SF);
  radio.setCodingRate(LORA_CR);
  radio.setSyncWord(LORA_SYNC);
  radio.setPreambleLength(LORA_PREAMBLE);
  radio.setCRC(true);
  radio.setRxBoostedGainMode(true);   // <<< +2-3 dB sensitivity, key for range
  Serial.print(F("[RF] Listening on ")); Serial.print(f, 3); Serial.println(F(" MHz"));
}
void setup() {
  Serial.begin(115200);
  delay(500);
  Serial.println();
  Serial.println(F("===== VRITRA TEAM 025 RECEIVER ====="));
  loraSPI.begin(18, 19, 23, NSS_PIN);
  Serial.print(F("[LR1121] init... "));
  int st = radio.begin();
  if (st != RADIOLIB_ERR_NONE) {
    Serial.print(F("FAIL code=")); Serial.println(st);
    while (1) delay(100);
  }
  Serial.println(F("OK"));
  radio.setRfSwitchTable(rfswitch_pins, rfswitch_table);
  radio.setTCXO(2.7, 5000);
  applyRxConfig();
  radio.setPacketReceivedAction(onRx);
  radio.startReceive();
  Serial.println(F("=== READY — waiting for packets ==="));
  Serial.println(F("Send 'b' + Enter to toggle to backup freq (865.75)"));
  Serial.println(F("Send 'm' + Enter to return to main   (867.00)"));
}
void loop() {
  // Serial commands to switch main/backup freq
  if (Serial.available()) {
    char c = Serial.read();
    if (c == 'b' || c == 'B') { useBackupFreq = true;  applyRxConfig(); radio.startReceive(); }
    if (c == 'm' || c == 'M') { useBackupFreq = false; applyRxConfig(); radio.startReceive(); }
  }
  if (!packetFlag) return;
  packetFlag = false;
  String msg;
  int st = radio.readData(msg);
  if (st == RADIOLIB_ERR_NONE) {
    goodCount++;
    float rssi = radio.getRSSI();
    float snr  = radio.getSNR();
    float ferr = radio.getFrequencyError();
    Serial.print(F("RX #")); Serial.print(goodCount);
    Serial.print(F(" | RSSI=")); Serial.print(rssi, 1); Serial.print(F(" dBm"));
    Serial.print(F(" | SNR="));  Serial.print(snr, 1);  Serial.print(F(" dB"));
    Serial.print(F(" | fErr=")); Serial.print(ferr, 0); Serial.print(F(" Hz"));
    Serial.print(F(" | len=")); Serial.print(msg.length());
    Serial.print(F(" | \"")); Serial.print(msg); Serial.println(F("\""));
    // PER stats every 20 packets
    if (goodCount % 20 == 0) {
      Serial.print(F("--- STATS: good=")); Serial.print(goodCount);
      Serial.print(F(" crcErr=")); Serial.println(crcErrCount);
    }
  } else if (st == RADIOLIB_ERR_CRC_MISMATCH) {
    crcErrCount++;
    Serial.print(F("[CRC ERR] RSSI="));
    Serial.print(radio.getRSSI(), 1); Serial.println(F(" dBm"));
  }
  radio.startReceive();
}
Delete these
The lr1121_config.h and lr1121_config.cpp in both folders — they reference wavesahre_lora_1121.h (Waveshare's raw HAL) but are never included by your .ino files. RadioLib doesn't need them. They just cause confusion. Remove both files from both folders.
Diagnostic checklist if you're still not getting 2 km
Run through in this order:
Bench test at 1 m — RSSI should read around -40 to -50 dBm. If it's -70+ at 1 m, one radio has an antenna/matching problem.
Frequency error (fErr) — should be under ±2 kHz. If it's ±20 kHz+, TCXO isn't calibrated → power-cycle both boards, verify setTCXO(2.7, 5000) succeeded.
Antenna VSWR — get a NanoVNA on each whip at 867 MHz. Should read VSWR under 2:1. Most "10 cm 868 MHz whips" from robu are actually cut for 915 MHz.
Ground plane — TX-side whip needs a metal ground plane or 4 radials, or the pattern is a mess and you lose 6+ dB skyward.
RX height — get the Yagi at least 3 m up on a mast, pointed at the cansat.
Line of sight — the Fresnel zone at 2 km / 867 MHz needs ~9 m clearance at midpoint. If there's a tree or building, you'll lose 20+ dB.
If SNR is above -5 dB at 2 km, RSSI above -100 dBm, and you're still losing packets → it's a firmware/timing bug, not RF. Post the RX log and I'll debug.
error in transmission codeC:\Users\kalbh\Downloads\Transmitter\Transmitter.ino: In function 'void applyRadioConfig()':
C:\Users\kalbh\Downloads\Transmitter\Transmitter.ino:58:9: error: 'class LR1121' has no member named 'setCurrentLimit'
   58 |   radio.setCurrentLimit(140);   // headroom for HP PA
      |         ^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
exit status 1
Compilation error: 'class LR1121' has no member named 'setCurrentLimit'
The LR1121 RadioLib class doesn't expose setCurrentLimit() (that's an SX127x/SX126x-family method — LR1121 handles PA current limiting internally via setOutputPower() and the HP PA config). Just delete that line.
Ran a command
Ran a command
Fix — in applyRadioConfig(), remove this line:
cpp
radio.setCurrentLimit(140);   // headroom for HP PA
So the function becomes:
cpp
void applyRadioConfig() {
  float f = useBackupFreq ? FREQ_BACKUP : FREQ_MAIN;
  radio.setFrequency(f);
  radio.setBandwidth(LORA_BW);
  radio.setSpreadingFactor(LORA_SF);
  radio.setCodingRate(LORA_CR);
  radio.setSyncWord(LORA_SYNC);
  radio.setPreambleLength(LORA_PREAMBLE);
  radio.setCRC(true);
  radio.setOutputPower(TX_POWER_DBM);
  Serial.print(F("[RF] freq=")); Serial.print(f, 3);
  Serial.print(F(" MHz SF")); Serial.print(LORA_SF);
  Serial.print(F(" BW")); Serial.print(LORA_BW, 0);
  Serial.print(F(" CR4/")); Serial.print(LORA_CR);
  Serial.print(F(" Sync=0x")); Serial.print(LORA_SYNC, HEX);
  Serial.print(F(" Pwr=")); Serial.print(TX_POWER_DBM); Serial.println(F("dBm"));
}
That's the only reference to setCurrentLimit in either file (transmitter or receiver), so this one deletion should get you compiling. Try it and let me know if you hit another error.

Read your zip, the frequency plan, the RadioLib LR11x0/LR1121 API docs, and the relevant RadioLib issue threads. I found the bug, and it was mine.
Root cause
On the LR1121 there are two physically separate transmit pins:
RFO_HP_LF — high-power path, up to +22 dBm
RFO_LP_LF — low-power/high-efficiency path, up to +15 dBm
The LR1121 datasheet lists a high power PA path of +22 dBm on pin RFO_HP_LF and a high efficiency PA path of +15 dBm on pin RFO_LP_LF for sub-GHz. 
eBay
 Your RF switch table routes them differently: MODE_TX = {HIGH, HIGH} (LP), MODE_TX_HP = {LOW, HIGH} (HP).
Now here's the killer. RadioLib picks the PA based on the power number you pass: allowed values are -9 to 22 dBm on the high-power PA and -17 to 14 dBm on the low-power PA. 
GitHub
 Anything ≤14 dBm silently goes out the LP pin.
Your original code used setOutputPower(22) → HP path → the pin that's actually matched and connected on the Core1121. I changed it to setOutputPower(14) for legal compliance → LP path → an unmatched/likely-unconnected pin. That's easily 30–40 dB of loss, which is exactly 2 km → 40 m.
The fix: setOutputPower(14, true) — the second argument forces the HP PA. You keep the legal +14 dBm but out the correct pin. LR1121 inherits setOutputPower(int8_t power, bool forceHighPower, uint32_t rampTimeUs) from LR1120, so this compiles.
Two other things I got wrong: I added setTCXO(2.7, 5000) — the Core1121 has an onboard TCXO but RadioLib's default is 1.6 V, and forcing 2.7 V can destabilize the PLL. And none of the config calls were return-checked, so a silent failure looked like a working radio.
Let me build you clean, fully-instrumented versions.
Created 2 files, read a file
Created 2 files, read a file
Delete these first
lr1121_config.h and lr1121_config.cpp in both folders. They #include "wavesahre_lora_1121.h" (Waveshare's bare-metal HAL) but are never included by either .ino. They're dead files creating false confidence — the values in them (14 dBm, LORA_SYNCWORD 0x12, ENABLE_RX_BOOST_MODE false) do nothing. RadioLib does all of it.
What changed
Item	Before	Now
PA path	LP pin (unmatched)	HP pin, forced
Error checking	none	every call reported
TCXO	forced 2.7 V	1.6 V (RadioLib/Core1121 default)
RX boost	off	on (+2–3 dB free)
Switch table	5 modes	7 modes, set before and after begin()
CRC	setCRC(true) → 1 byte	setCRC(2) → 2 bytes, stronger
Test flow	type on phone	auto-beacon every 3 s for walking a range test
Diagnostics	none	seq numbers, PER, RSSI/SNR/freq-error, PA A/B mode
Do this in order — 20 minutes total
1. Bench test, radios 1–2 m apart, antennas connected.
Flash both. RX should print something like:
[RX ] #1 seq=1 PA=H RSSI=-38.5 SNR=9.8 fErr=-412Hz len=18  "V025,1,H,14,BEACON"
Expected values at 1 m:
RSSI −30 to −50 dBm → RF path is healthy. Go to step 3.
RSSI −70 or worse → still ~30 dB of loss somewhere. Go to step 2.
fErr above ±3000 Hz → TCXO problem. Set TCXO_VOLTAGE to 0.0f on both and retry.
Nothing at all → check the begin() code printed at boot.
2. If RSSI is still bad, run the PA A/B test. Type /patest on in the TX serial monitor. It now alternates HP and LP pins each packet and tags them. Watch the [SUM] line on RX:
[PA TEST] HP best RSSI -38.2 (14 rx)   LP best RSSI -71.0 (14 rx)
That 30+ dB gap confirms the diagnosis and confirms HP is your working path. If HP and LP are both bad, the problem is not the PA selection — it's the antenna. Which brings us to:
3. Check the antenna connector. The Core1121 has two IPEX4 connectors — one sub-GHz, one 2.4 GHz. If your 867 MHz antenna is plugged into the 2.4 GHz port, you get exactly the 30–40 m you're seeing, and no code change will ever fix it. Verify against the Waveshare Core1121 wiki pinout before anything else. This is the single most common cause of this symptom and it costs nothing to check.
4. Then walk the range test. Beacon runs on its own every 3 s, so one person stays with the RX laptop and one walks. Yagi on a tripod, at least 2–3 m up, pointed at the TX. Watch RSSI degrade smoothly with distance — if it falls off a cliff rather than smoothly, that's an obstruction or a Fresnel zone problem, not the radio.
Sanity check on the target
At mandated SF10/BW125/CR4/5 and +14 dBm, with your 2 dBi whip and the 13 dBi Yagi from your PDR:
+14 dBm TX  +2 dBi  -97.2 dB (FSPL @ 2 km)  +13 dBi  =  -68 dBm at RX
SF10/BW125 sensitivity with RxBoost:            ~ -134 dBm
                                     MARGIN:      66 dB
You are not remotely power-limited at 2 km. If the link fails after these fixes it is a hardware fault, not a settings problem — and the RSSI/SNR/fErr numbers the RX now prints will tell us exactly which.
One thing to note for later (not now): at SF10/BW125 a 40-byte packet is ~535 ms of airtime, so 1 Hz telemetry is >50% duty cycle. That's a CDR problem to solve with shorter binary packets, not an ASCII string. Flag it but don't let it block you today.

Show more
TX side is solid — 65 packets transmitted, HP path confirmed working. But RX shows zero receptions in 210 seconds, even in the PA test window. At bench range this isn't a "weak signal" problem — it's a total receive failure. And the -127.5 dBm noise floor repeating identically every single time is suspicious: real ambient RF noise fluctuates. That constant number, plus two anomalous 0.0 dBm readings, points to the noise-floor read itself failing/returning a fixed error-adjacent value — which usually means the RX radio is not actually sitting in an active RX state when I sample it, or SPI is glitching.
First, send me the RX boot log
I need to see what printed at RX startup — specifically:
[RF ] chip HW ... device ... fw ...
[RF ] listening 867.00 MHz  SF10  BW125k ...
If begin() or startReceive() threw an error code, my RFC() wrapper would have printed it — did it? Paste the full RX serial output from power-up.
Meanwhile, run this bypass test — it isolates the bug in 30 seconds
This throws out the interrupt entirely and does a dumb blocking receive. If this catches packets, the problem is your IRQ pin/callback wiring. If it catches nothing either, the problem is RF/antenna on the RX side.
Created a file, read a file
Created a file, read a file
While that runs, check these physically — most likely culprits given zero receptions at bench range
Antenna on the RX board. Is it actually screwed onto the sub-GHz IPEX4/SMA port, not the 2.4 GHz one, not left disconnected?
Is the RX board even the LR1121, or did you accidentally grab a different dev board for the second unit? Worth a literal visual check.
Power. Is the RX ESP32 getting clean 3.3V, same USB cable/port type as TX? A brownout during SPI transactions can make begin() succeed (it's just register writes) while receive silently never triggers.
Ground reference — if TX and RX are powered from different USB hubs/laptops with no common ground and you're testing at very close range, weird coupling effects can happen, but this is a distant possibility next to 1–3.
What to send me next
Full RX boot log from your actual Receiver.ino (not just the loop) — I need to see if begin() and startReceive() printed OK.
Output from this RX_Diagnostic.ino after ~30–60 seconds running, TX still beaconing every 3 s nearby.
If the diagnostic also shows nothing, next I'll have you flip TX and RX roles between the two boards (swap which physical board runs Transmitter.ino vs Receiver.ino) — that isolates whether one specific board has a hardware fault, independent of firmware.

That's it — link is alive and clean. RSSI −30 to −46 dBm, SNR 13–15 dB, 0% packet loss over 18 packets, fErr=0Hz (TCXO locked perfectly). The antenna was the whole problem, exactly as suspected — wrong IPEX4 port.
Now go get your actual 2 km number
1. Turn off PA test mode — you don't need it anymore, it was just to catch the antenna issue:
/patest off
2. Take it outside, walk the range. Beacon is already running every 3 s, so:
RX stays put on the tripod with the Yagi, pointed toward where TX will walk/drive
TX goes with you, antenna vertical, away from your body if possible (hand/body absorbs a surprising amount at 867 MHz)
Watch RSSI in real time — log distances at 100 m, 500 m, 1 km, 1.5 km, 2 km checkpoints if you can, either by eye on a map or GPS on your phone
3. What to expect / red flags:
RSSI at 2 km	Meaning
−90 to −110 dBm	Healthy, matches your PDR link budget, tons of margin left
−115 to −125 dBm	Getting close to the floor but should still work (SF10 sensitivity ~−132 dBm, +boost ~−134)
No packets / RSSI pegged at noise floor	LOS obstruction (trees, buildings, curvature) — check Fresnel clearance, raise the Yagi
If RSSI degrades smoothly as you walk away, everything is working as designed — that's just physics, not a bug. If it's fine at 1.5 km then drops off a cliff at 1.8 km, that's almost always a line-of-sight obstruction at that specific spot, not the radio.
4. Reset stats before each new checkpoint so PER numbers mean something — on the RX serial, press r.
One thing to fix before flight (not today, but note it)

Analyzed the full log. Let me give you the honest engineering picture before the code, because the code is not your main problem anymore — and if I hand you a new sketch without saying that, you'll burn another two days.
Forensic analysis of the log
1. Your received power is ~35 dB below where it should be.
At 418 m, 867 MHz, free-space path loss is 83.6 dB. With +14 dBm TX, 2 dBi whip, and your PDR's 13 dBi Yagi, you should be seeing −55 dBm. Even with a plain whip on both ends: −66 dBm. You measured −97 to −100 dBm.
That 35 dB gap is the entire problem, and it's geometry, not firmware.
2. Your noise floor is ~20 dB above thermal.
Thermal floor at BW125 with the LR1121's noise figure is about −117 dBm. Your idle readings show −96 to −108 dBm. That's 10–20 dB of excess noise — laptop switching supply, USB cable acting as an antenna, RX antenna sitting next to digital electronics. Every dB of excess noise is a dB of range taken directly off the top.
3. SNR proves you were at the demodulation cliff.
SF10's demod floor is about −15 dB SNR. Your log shows −13, −15, −17, −20.8. You weren't "at 418 m" — you were at the edge of decodability because of items 1 and 2. PER climbed 7% → 10% → 17% → 22% exactly as SNR crossed the threshold.
4. Two-ray ground reflection, not free space.
Breakpoint distance = 4·h₁·h₂/λ. At λ=0.346 m with both antennas at ~1.5 m, that's 26 m. Beyond 26 m you are in the d⁴ regime, not d². Path loss at 418 m becomes ~98 dB instead of 84 dB. Antenna height is the single highest-leverage variable you have — and in d⁴, doubling height gains 12 dB, which doubles range.
5. You ran the test backwards.
The Yagi's job is to sit fixed on the ground station. You put the fixed unit on TX and walked the RX — which means you walked the end that needed the 13 dBi antenna and the stable power, and you walked it at hand height next to a human body (5–10 dB absorption) attached to a laptop (noise source).
6. Five POWERON_RESETs on the receiver.
rst:0x1 (POWERON_RESET),boot:0x13 (SPI_FAST_FLASH_BOOT)
rst:0x1 is not a software crash. A watchdog gives TG0WDT_SYS_RESET; a panic gives SW_CPU_RESET. POWERON_RESET means VCC physically dropped below the brownout threshold. Walking with a USB-tethered board did this five times, then dropped the port entirely ("Connection lost").
Two real firmware bugs I did find
Bug A — explicit header lets garbage through as valid packets.
Look at these lines:
[RX ] #95  seq=0 PA=? SNR=-20.8  "V^Pc^V^I?grP?(?^M^I?O??^D"
[RX ] #128 seq=0 PA=? SNR=-20.5  "V026^_1^U^[^NH,?<$??Ac|/"
Those were reported as ERR_NONE — CRC passed — yet the content is noise. That is not possible with a working CRC.
The cause: in explicit header mode, the receiver obeys the CRC-present flag and payload length carried in the received header, not its own configuration. At SNR below −15 dB the header itself misdecodes, can declare "CRC off, length 20," and RadioLib then hands you 20 bytes of unchecked noise as a good packet. Your PER numbers are also corrupted by this — some "good" packets were noise.
Fix: implicit header with fixed length. The receiver then uses its own length and CRC settings, garbage gets rejected, and you drop the header symbols too.
Bug B — my idle noise-floor read was disarming your receiver.
cpp
Serial.print(radio.getRSSI(false), 1);
getRSSI(bool packet, bool skipReceive=false) — with skipReceive defaulting to false, this call changes the radio's operating state and I never re-armed with startReceive(). That's the source of the alternating −127.5 / 0.0 garbage readings, and it may have cost you packets during idle gaps. My mistake.
Now the final code, with both fixed.

Where the 2 km actually comes from
I have to be straight with you about the budget, because you asked for the best possible code and I've now given you that — but code is worth maybe 4–6 dB here, and you need about 35.
Change	Gain	Type
Implicit header + CRC8 (false packets eliminated, true PER visible)	~2 dB effective	firmware ✅ done
RxBoost already on	+2–3 dB	firmware ✅ done
Swap the roles: Yagi on the FIXED receiver	+11 dB	setup
Raise both antennas 1 m → 3 m	+9 dB (d⁴ regime)	setup
TX antenna clear of the operator's body	+5–10 dB	setup
RX antenna away from laptop, ferrite on USB	+5–15 dB (noise floor)	setup
Firmware total: ~5 dB. Setup total: ~30–45 dB. In the d⁴ two-ray regime every 12 dB doubles your range, so +30 dB is roughly a 5.6× improvement — 418 m × 5.6 ≈ 2.3 km. That's your 2 km, and it comes almost entirely from how you run the test.
Test protocol — run it exactly this way
Ground station (fixed):
Receiver board + Yagi on the tripod, at least 3 m up, pointed down the walking line
Powered from a power bank, not the laptop — that kills the brownout resets
Laptop for serial, but keep the antenna ≥1 m away from the laptop and coil a ferrite on the USB cable
Rover (walking):
Transmitter board + whip, held overhead or on a 2 m pole, vertical, away from the body
Powered from its own power bank
Phone paired over Bluetooth so you can confirm TX is alive without a laptop
Procedure:
Press n at the start to log your noise floor. If it's worse than −110 dBm, fix that before walking — you're leaving range on the table.
Press r at each checkpoint (200 m, 500 m, 1 km, 1.5 km, 2 km) so PER is per-segment.
Walk slowly, stop at each checkpoint for ~10 packets (30 s).
Copy the CSV, lines into Excel afterwards and plot RSSI vs distance.
The number that tells you everything
Watch the margin= field. It's your SNR above SF10's demodulation floor:
margin > +10 dB → solid, keep walking
margin +3 to +10 dB → working, approaching the edge
margin < +3 dB → you're at the cliff; that's your max range for this configuration
If margin is still low at 500 m with the Yagi up, stop walking and fix the noise floor — don't keep going.
One thing that will bite you at CDR
Your 24-byte packet is ~400 ms airtime at the mandated SF10/BW125. At 1 Hz telemetry that's 40% duty cycle against a 1% WPC limit. The binary struct I gave you is the right foundation, but the flight schema needs to be tighter and slower. Separate problem, separate day — don't let it stop you finishing the range test.

Understood — no Yagi, two rubber ducks. Let me tell you what that actually costs you and what firmware can claw back.
The math with 2 dBi at both ends
Free space at 2 km: received = 14 + 2 − 97.2 + 2 = −79 dBm. Against SF10 sensitivity of −134 dBm that's 55 dB of margin. Trivially easy.
But you are not in free space. Two-ray ground reflection takes over past the breakpoint distance 4·h₁·h₂/λ:
Antenna heights	Breakpoint	Path loss @ 2 km	Received	Margin
1.5 m / 1.5 m	26 m	125 dB	−107 dBm	27 dB ✅
3 m / 3 m	104 m	113 dB	−95 dBm	39 dB ✅
10 m / 1.5 m (rooftop RX)	173 m	108 dB	−90 dBm	43 dB ✅
2 km is achievable with your rubber ducks. All three cases close the link. So why did you only get 418 m?
Back-calculating from your log: reported RSSI −97 with SNR −13 means true signal power ≈ −110 dBm at 418 m. Two-ray at 1.5 m predicts −80 dBm there. You had 30 dB of unexplained loss. That's:
Cross-polarization: up to 20 dB. If one antenna was tilted or horizontal while the other was vertical, that alone destroys the link. Rubber ducks are strictly vertical-polarized.
Human body absorption: 5–10 dB. Holding the unit against your chest.
Detuning: a rubber duck pressed against a laptop, a hand, or a metal surface shifts its resonance off 867 MHz.
None of that is code. But there are real firmware levers left, and I've now used all of them.

New firmware levers in this build
Change	Gain	Where
WiFi + BT killed on receiver	3–10 dB (attacks your −97 dBm noise floor)	RX
CPU dropped to 80 MHz	1–3 dB less self-generated noise	RX
LDO instead of DC-DC regulator	~0.5–1 dB quieter front end	RX
Payload 24 → 20 bytes (28 vs 33 symbols)	1–2 dB effective at the cliff	both
Optional dual-send (/dual on)	~3–4 dB apparent	TX
Optional BT off (ENABLE_BT 0)	less supply sag during the PA burst	TX
Realistic firmware total: 8–18 dB, most of it from the noise floor. That's genuinely significant — in the d⁴ ground-reflection regime, 12 dB doubles your range. 418 m → ~840 m from firmware alone.
The rest has to come from how you hold the antennas
Polarization is your biggest free win. Both rubber ducks must be vertical, same orientation, at both ends, at all times. Tilt one 90° and you lose up to 20 dB instantly. Your walker almost certainly had it angled.
Height is your second. From the table above, going from 1.5 m to 3 m at both ends buys 12 dB — a doubling of range in the d⁴ regime. Tape both boards to 3 m PVC/bamboo poles. Cheap, and worth more than any antenna upgrade.
If you can put the receiver on a rooftop or upper floor, 10 m up, you get −90 dBm at 2 km with 43 dB of margin, and you'll clear it comfortably.

Current packet length is 20 bytes.
typedef struct __attribute__((packed)) {
  uint8_t  magic;           // 1 byte
  uint8_t  teamId;          // 1 byte
  uint32_t seq;             // 4 bytes
  uint32_t upMs;            // 4 bytes
  int8_t   txPower;         // 1 byte
  uint8_t  paPath;          // 1 byte
  uint8_t  flags;           // 1 byte
  char     msg[6];          // 6 bytes (PKT_MSG_LEN)
  uint8_t  crc8;            // 1 byte
} RangePkt_t;              // == 20 bytes total
Airtime at SF10/BW125/CR4-5: approximately 370 ms per packet.
At 1 Hz telemetry (one packet per second), that's 37% duty cycle — way above the 1% WPC limit. For flight you'll need to either:
Send at lower frequency (e.g., 0.1 Hz = one packet every 10 seconds = 3.7% still high, need ~0.03 Hz or lower)
Use a tighter binary format (e.g., 8 bytes instead of 20)
Implement a more sophisticated compression or data batching scheme
For the range walk today, 370 ms / packet is fine — you're testing RF link, not production duty cycle. But note it for your CDR.

OK. Your PDR telemetry spec is 20 fields, but you're transmitting CSV ASCII text, which is the competition requirement for the actual flight. That format will NOT fit in a 20-byte binary packet.
Here's the real situation:
Competition requirement (flight/CDR)
You must transmit as ASCII comma-separated fields:
<TEAM_ID>,<TIME>,<PACKET_COUNT>,<ALTITUDE>,<PRESSURE>,<TEMP>,<VOLTAGE>,
<GNSS_TIME>,<GNSS_LAT>,<GNSS_LON>,<GNSS_ALT>,<GNSS_SATS>,
<ACCEL>,<GYRO>,<FLIGHT_STATE>,<OPTIONAL>
Minimum size: roughly 60–100 bytes depending on how many decimals you send.
Airtime at SF10/BW125: ~800 ms – 1.2 seconds per packet.
At 1 Hz: 80–120% duty cycle — violates the 1% WPC limit by 80–120×.
What I built (range walk only)
The 20-byte binary packet I gave you is for testing RF link only, not for the actual flight. It's not competition-legal because it's not ASCII.
What you actually need for CDR
Before your next test, you have a CDR-stage software problem to solve that's bigger than the LoRa RF:
Binary telemetry format (not ASCII). Pack your 20 fields into 32–48 bytes with fixed-point math (altitude as int16 decimetres, pressure as uint16 Pa, lat/lon as int32 ten-millionths of a degree, etc.).
Transmit at ~0.1 Hz (one packet every 10 seconds) to meet the 1% duty cycle. At 370 ms airtime, that's 37% duty per 10-second window → still 3.7% averaged. You need to go slower or make the packet smaller.
Or use LR-FHSS mode (Semtech's frequency-hopping scheme) which has lower duty-cycle penalties, but that's a different modulation and your receiver needs support.
For today's 2 km range walk, stick with the 20-byte binary packet. It's clean, debuggable, and lets you prove the RF link works.
For CDR, you'll need to:
Redesign the packet in binary format
Implement a duty-cycle respecting transmit scheduler
Validate it against the 1% WPC rule in your calculations
Which do you want to tackle first — finish the range walk with the current code, or start the CDR packet redesign now?

Remaining 7 environmental sensors — unblocks payload science tests, lowest urgency for CDR since these are typically demonstrated individually, not as a full integration

Don't i have any sensors which will display data
Looking at what's actually in hand right now, here's the honest answer:
Sensors you have that CAN display data today
Sensor	What it measures	Blocker?
BMP585 ×3	Pressure, altitude, temperature	None — I2C, 3.3V, works standalone
L89 GPS ×2	Position, altitude, time, satellite count	None — UART, needs outdoor sky view
DS3231 RTC	Time only (not really "sensor data" but does report)	None
That's it. BMP585 and L89 GPS are your only two sensors that will produce live data right now — everything else you have is either not a sensor (Teensy, buck converters, TB6612FNG, flash) or is a sensor that's blocked:
Sensor you have but CANNOT read yet
ICM-20948 — you have 2 of these, but it needs a dedicated 1.8V VDDIO rail via AMS1117-1.8, which you haven't ordered. Without that regulator, this IMU will not initialize over I2C. This is your only "have it but can't use it" sensor.
Everything else that reads sensor data — not yet ordered
SCD41 (CO2), HDC3020 (humidity), ML8511 (UV), MQ131 (ozone), BMV080 (PM), AS7343 (spectral), SEN0290 (lightning), LSM6DSO32 (accel/gyro) — none of these are in your bill yet.
What I'd do right now
Since BMP585 and L89 GPS are your only working sensors today, that's actually a legitimate CDR demo:
Wire up one BMP585 to a Teensy 4.1, read live pressure/altitude/temp over I2C, print to serial
Wire up one L89 GPS, get a cold fix outdoors, log lat/lon/alt/sats
That's a real "sensor produces live data" test you can run this afternoon with zero new orders. Want the Teensy code for either of those?
wrror in transmitting code Sketch uses 1641863 bytes (125%) of program storage space. Maximum is 1310720 bytes.
Global variables use 62648 bytes (19%) of dynamic memory, leaving 265032 bytes for local variables. Maximum is 327680 bytes.

text section exceeds available space in board
Compilation error: text section exceeds available space in board'
This isn't a code-logic error — it's a partition scheme setting in the Arduino IDE. Your sketch (1.64 MB) is genuinely bigger than the flash partition currently allocated to your app (1.31 MB), because RadioLib + BluetoothSerial classic BT stack together are large.
Fix — increase the flash partition (fastest, do this first)
In Arduino IDE:
Tools → Partition Scheme
Change from whatever it's currently set to (likely "Default" ~1.3MB app / OTA) to:
"No OTA (2MB APP/2MB SPIFFS)", or
"Huge APP (3MB No OTA/1MB SPIFFS)"
This alone should fix it — your board almost certainly has 4MB flash total, and the default scheme reserves space for OTA updates and SPIFFS you're not using.
If that option isn't available, or you want a smaller binary anyway
Bluetooth Classic (BluetoothSerial.h) is the single biggest thing bloating this binary — it pulls in the full Bluedroid stack. Two options:
Option A — disable BT for this build (you already have the switch):
cpp
#define ENABLE_BT   0
Set that to 0 and reflash. This should shrink the binary by several hundred KB, likely under the 1.31MB limit even with the default partition scheme.
Option B — keep BT, just change the partition scheme. Since you're actively range-testing and probably want phone control, do this instead of disabling BT.
Recommended for your case
Since you're mid-range-test and want Bluetooth control from your phone, go with the partition scheme change — Tools → Partition Scheme → "Huge APP (3MB No OTA/1MB SPIFFS)" — reflash, and it should compile clean.
If your board doesn't show that option (some ESP32 board definitions hide it), tell me the exact board you selected under Tools → Board, and I'll give you the exact alternative.

This is excellent — proceed to the long-range test.
What this log tells you
0% packet error rate, 30+ packets, zero CRC failures, zero bad magic — link integrity is clean
RSSI −26 to −70 dBm at 1 m — that spread is just you or your friend moving/rotating the antenna during the bench test, not a problem
SNR 10–15 dB, margin 25–30 dB — you have massive headroom above the SF10 demodulation floor
fErr=0Hz — TCXO is locked, frequency stable
Sequence reset to 1 partway through — that's just a TX reboot (Bluetooth flash re-upload probably), harmless, not a bug
Bench test is fully validated. This is the "everything is healthy" baseline you needed before walking.
Before you go, quick checklist
Note this baseline: −26 to −45 dBm is your near-field reference. As you walk, expect a smooth decline, not a cliff.
/dual on if you want the extra redundancy margin for the far checkpoints (optional, doubles airtime).
Press r on the RX right now to zero stats before you start walking, so your first real distance checkpoint isn't polluted by this bench data.
Antennas vertical, TX away from the body, RX up on whatever height you've got.
Walk checkpoints: 200 m → 500 m → 1 km → 1.5 km → 2 km, press r at each, wait ~10 packets, note RSSI/SNR/margin.
What to watch for as distance increases
Margin above +10 dB → solid link, keep going
Margin +3 to +10 dB → getting close to the edge but still working
Margin below +3 dB or packets stop → that's your real-world max range for this setup
Go walk it and send me the CSV lines from each checkpoint.
So, first, you need to thoroughly research everything required for the CanSat electronics system. My primary goal is to design a professional schematic and PCB. However, I do not intend to build a fully custom PCB where every module is broken down into individual ICs, resistors, capacitors, and supporting circuitry.
Instead, the PCB should be modular. The PCB will act as the central backbone for power distribution, signal routing, connectors, protection circuits, and mechanical mounting. The actual modules (GPS, sensors, radio module, ESP32, Raspberry Pi, IMU, power modules, etc.) will remain intact and will be connected directly to the PCB through headers, sockets, or dedicated connectors. This approach will allow any damaged module to be removed and replaced easily without soldering individual components or reworking the PCB.
What I need is a complete PCB design process from the beginning, including:
Understanding the complete hardware architecture of the CanSat.
Creating a professional schematic.
Designing a clean, organized PCB with proper routing.
Following industry-standard design practices.
Avoiding clutter, unnecessary wiring complexity, and poor component placement.
Receiving step-by-step instructions for every stage of the design process.
Before creating the PCB design guide, I want you to thoroughly study and understand KiCad 10.0. Review its official documentation, recommended workflows, professional PCB design practices, and relevant learning resources. Then, create instructions specifically tailored to KiCad 10.0 and to the requirements of this CanSat project.
The first task is to determine the complete hardware architecture and generate a detailed list of all components that will likely be used in the CanSat system. For each component, provide:
Component name
Quantity required
Purpose and functionality
Interface type
Power requirements
Reason for selection
Recommended connector or mounting method
After reviewing that component list, I will make any necessary changes or additions. Only then should the final schematic and PCB design guide be created.
The PCB must be designed to professional standards. If connectors are required, evaluate all possible options and select the most reliable and serviceable solution. Consider whether manufacturer-provided footprints, custom footprints, board-to-board connectors, locking connectors, screw terminals, JST connectors, or other alternatives would provide a better design. Do not choose shortcuts simply because they are easier to implement.
The internal mounting plate available for the PCB has a radius of 11 cm. All component placement, connector positioning, mounting holes, and routing strategies must be planned around this mechanical constraint. If the design would benefit from multiple interconnected PCBs instead of a single board, that option should also be evaluated.
Power connections must be serviceable. For example, instead of soldering battery wires directly to the PCB, I want a dedicated screw-terminal connector or equivalent high-reliability connector mounted on the PCB. The Molicel battery leads should connect to this terminal, allowing easy installation, removal, maintenance, and replacement.
The final PCB layout should fit comfortably within the available dimensions. The design should not be congested, but it should also avoid wasting space. Component placement, routing, and connector orientation should be optimized for both manufacturability and maintenance.
Approach this task as a senior hardware design engineer with more than 30 years of professional PCB design experience. Every decision should prioritize reliability, maintainability, mechanical robustness, electrical performance, ease of assembly, and professional engineering standards.
My objective is to create the best possible KiCad-based CanSat PCB design with a modular architecture, professional documentation, and a clear path from schematic design to final PCB manufacturing. do this task carefully

Searched memory
OK — enough context. Before I draw anything, I need to lock down 12 things. Answer these and I'll build the component list your way; guess and I'll build the wrong PCB.
Some of these have preferred answers already implied by your PDR or by good engineering judgment — I've marked those with "Recommended:". If you say "use recommended for all," I'll proceed with those.
Section A — Mechanical constraints (design-shaping)
A1. Radius vs diameter. You said 11 cm radius = 22 cm diameter. But your PDR body is 130 mm (13 cm) diameter carbon fiber. A 22 cm PCB physically cannot fit in a 13 cm body. Which is correct — is the plate radius 11 cm / diameter 22 cm, or radius 55 mm / diameter 110 mm? I strongly suspect you meant diameter 110 mm (radius 55 mm). Confirm.
A2. Stack architecture. Your PDR shows "multiple circular plates connected using spacers." Is this ONE PCB carrying everything, or a stack of 2–3 boards connected by board-to-board headers? A single 110 mm circular PCB is very tight for your ~15 modules + 3 buck converters + battery terminal + 2× LR1121 + 4× servo drivers. A stacked design would be professional standard for this density.
Recommended: 2-board stack (Power/Actuation board + Avionics/Sensors board), interconnected via a keyed board-to-board header.
A3. Board thickness and layer count. Standard 1.6 mm 2-layer is cheapest and easy. For dense signal routing and a solid ground plane under RF modules, 4-layer 1.6 mm is the professional norm — costs ~2× at prototype quantities (~₹2500 vs ₹1200 for 5 boards at JLCPCB).
Recommended: 4-layer, 1.6 mm, ENIG finish (gold, better solderability, needed for reliable header contact).
A4. Mounting hole pattern. How many mounting holes, what diameter, what bolt circle? Your PDR mentions plates on spacers — I need the exact hole positions/diameters to align with your mechanical CAD.
Recommended pending your CAD: 4× M3 clearance (Ø3.2 mm) at 90° spacing on a 90 mm bolt circle centered on the board, keepout zones around them.
Section B — Which modules actually go on the flight board
Your PDR + orders include a lot of components. Not all of them fly on the same board. Confirm:
B1. Ground station modules (LR1121 #3, ESP32-P4, Yagi) — these go on a SEPARATE ground-station PCB, not the flight PCB, right?
Recommended: Yes, ground station is a separate board (or just a breadboard/dev-fixture, since it never flies).
B2. Which processors fly? You have Teensy 4.1 ×2 (primary+backup), XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense ×2, ESP32-S3 WROOM. Confirm the flight config:
Recommended flight stack: Teensy 4.1 primary + Teensy 4.1 backup + XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense (camera). Drop ESP32-S3 WROOM from flight — it duplicates the Teensy. Any objection?
B3. Backup radio. Your PDR mentions backup companion processor with LR1121. Confirm flight has 2× LR1121 on board (primary + backup), correct?
B4. Payload science sensors (SCD41, HDC3020, ML8511, MQ131, BMV080, AS7343, SEN0290) — do these all live on the same PCB as flight avionics, or on a separate payload sub-board that plugs in? MQ131 alone draws 200 mA at 5V continuously with a burn-in requirement — putting it on the flight board complicates thermal/power design.
Recommended: Payload sensors on a dedicated payload daughter-board that plugs into the main stack via a single 20-pin connector. Isolates dirty analog/gas sensors from clean digital avionics.
Section C — Power system
C1. BMS decision. Your recent memory says BMS removed — 3S Molicel direct to bucks. Confirm this is still the plan for CDR, and that overcurrent/undervoltage protection is being handled elsewhere (fuse + software cutoff via INA226).
Recommended: Add an inline automotive-style ATO blade fuse (5A or 7.5A) on the battery+ line, in a serviceable holder on the PCB. INA226 monitors current, Teensy shuts down bucks via EN pin if OV/UV/OC detected. Document this as your "no-BMS safety strategy" for CDR reviewers.
C2. Battery connector. You want serviceable, not soldered. Options:
Screw terminal (Phoenix MKDS 1.5/2) — very reliable, bulky, ~6 mm tall
XT30 or XT60 (you already have XT60PW from your BOM!) — RC-standard, rated 30A/60A, keyed, well-proven
JST-XH 2-pin — cheap but only 3A rated, not enough for peak servo draw
Anderson Powerpole — bulletproof but big
Recommended: XT60PW (you already own these). Rated 60A continuous, keyed against reverse polarity, PCB-mount version exists (XT60PW-M). Vertical or right-angle, take your pick.
C3. Rail architecture. From memory: 3.3V (AP62301), 5V (AP64500), 7.4V adjustable (for servos). Confirm all three rails still needed, and that the ICM-20948's 1.8V VDDIO is a local point-of-load LDO (AMS1117-1.8) fed from 3.3V, on-board, not a separate rail.
C4. Power switch. How does the CanSat power on? Hard switch (KCD1 you ordered), remove-before-flight pin, or magnetic reed switch?
Recommended: KCD1 on battery+ line (you own these) as master switch, plus a remove-before-flight (RBF) pin in series that keeps the bucks disabled until the pin is pulled — competition safety norm.
Section D — Servos and actuation
D1. Servo count and connector. 2× AGFRC A20BHM (parafoil brakes, 8.4V), 2× MG90S (5V), 2× OT5330M, 1× GA12-N20 motor. That's 6 servos + 1 motor = 7 PWM channels. Standard servo pin header (3-pin JST-XH or 0.1" male header) per servo, or a dense multi-channel connector?
Recommended: Standard 3-pin 0.1" male headers per servo (SIG/V+/GND), locking JST-XH2.54 for the motor. Familiar, serviceable, cheap.
D2. Servo power isolation. Servos are noisy inductive loads. Do you want the servo bus (5V/7.4V) on its own physical connector from the buck, star-grounded separately from logic 3.3V/5V?
Recommended: Yes — separate power planes, single-point star ground under the primary Teensy, INA226 monitors both rails independently. Standard practice.
Section E — Data-plane connectors
E1. Teensy 4.1 mounting. Solder direct to PCB (permanent), or via female headers so it can be pulled out?
Recommended: Female stacking headers (2× 24-pin). Serviceability wins, cost is trivial, Teensy pops out for reflashing.
E2. Same question for XIAO ESP32-S3, ESP32-P4, LR1121 Core1121-HF modules, GPS L89, IMU, baro, RTC — all socketed via female headers?
Recommended: All socketed on female headers. This is exactly the modular philosophy you described. Every module removable, no soldering required.
E3. I2C bus topology. Multiple I2C sensors — one shared bus (address collisions?) or TCA9548A I2C mux? You have BMP585 ×2 or ×3 at address 0x76/0x77, ICM-20948 at 0x68/0x69, plus SCD41, HDC3020, etc.
Recommended: Add a TCA9548A I2C multiplexer on the sensor board. Solves BMP585 dual-address issue, keeps the bus clean, cheap. Not in your current BOM — add it to the next order.
Section F — External interfaces
F1. Antennas. LR1121 has IPEX4 connector already. Do you want the antenna cable to exit the PCB via a panel-mount SMA bulkhead wired to the LR1121's IPEX, or just the IPEX pigtail direct?
Recommended: Panel-mount SMA bulkhead on the PCB edge, IPEX-to-SMA pigtail internally. More robust — you can swap antennas without disturbing the LR1121 module.
F2. USB/UART access for on-pad reprogramming/debug. Do you want the Teensy USB accessible from outside the CanSat, or is a small access hatch OK?
Recommended: USB accessible via a small rectangular cutout in the shell aligned with the Teensy's USB port — no PCB-side connector needed, keeps design simple.
F3. Camera. XIAO ESP32-S3 Sense has an onboard camera connector. Camera lens must face outward — how is this mechanically aligned?
Depends on your CAD.
Section G — What I need from you to proceed
Answer these and I'll produce the component list. The fastest path is:
"Use recommended for all except: [list any you want changed]"
Or answer each individually. Once locked, I'll produce:
Complete flight-PCB BOM with quantities, purposes, interfaces, power, part numbers, and connector selections for each — in the format you asked for.
Payload daughter-board BOM (if we go with that architecture).
Ground-station BOM (separate).
Then you review, we lock, and I move to the KiCad schematic guide — which will be genuinely tailored to KiCad 10 workflows, not KiCad 7/8 tutorials rebadged.
One more thing before we start: KiCad 10.0 has not been released yet. The current stable release is KiCad 9.0 (as of early 2026). Do you actually mean KiCad 9.0? If a KiCad 10 has just been released, tell me — otherwise I'll write against KiCad 9.0 with notes on 10 when it drops.

Before I start writing 40+ pages of click-by-click, I need to be honest about scope so you're not surprised:
Realistic scope of "every click"
A complete professional schematic + PCB layout for a 3-board CanSat stack, done right in KiCad 10 with click-by-click detail, is roughly:
Schematic phase: ~800–1200 clicks total across 3 boards, ~50–80 pages of instructions
PCB layout phase: ~1500–2500 clicks total (component placement, routing, ground pours, DRC), ~80–120 pages
Total: effectively a small book, ~150–200 pages
I can deliver this — but it needs to come in staged phases you can actually work through, not one wall of text. Here's how I propose we structure it:
Proposed delivery structure
Phase 0 — Locked BOM v2 (deliver now, ~5 minutes to read)
The final 3-board BOM with all your latest changes applied. You confirm this once.
Phase 1 — KiCad 10 project setup (deliver next, ~15 minutes to work through)
Install/verify KiCad 10.0.5
Create the project folder structure
Set up custom symbol/footprint libraries
Configure PCB fab settings for JLCPCB 4-layer ENIG
Set up all 3 board files with 110 mm circular outlines
Phase 2 — Schematic: Board 1 (Power) (deliver after Phase 1, ~1 hour to work through)
Full click-by-click for Board 1's schematic. Simplest board, fewest components — gets you comfortable with the KiCad 10 workflow before Board 2's complexity.
Phase 3 — Schematic: Board 2 (CDH+Sensors) (deliver after Phase 2)
The dense one. All Teensys, LR1121, GPS, RTC, all sensors, TCA9548A, board-to-board headers.
Phase 4 — Schematic: Board 3 (Actuation) (deliver after Phase 3)
Teensy 4.0, servo headers, motor driver, INA226.
Phase 5 — PCB layout: all 3 boards (deliver after all schematics done)
Component placement on 110 mm circular boards, board-to-board header alignment, routing, ground pours, DRC.
Phase 6 — Fabrication prep (final)
Generate Gerbers, drill files, pick-and-place, upload to JLCPCB, order.
Why staged, not one big drop
Two reasons:
You need to work through each phase and hit problems — a KiCad step that doesn't behave as expected has to be diagnosed before moving forward. Delivering everything at once means you burn a week discovering issues in Phase 4 that would have been caught in Phase 1.
My memory of your project doesn't survive between chats — if you come back later with "I got stuck on step 47," I need context of what step 47 actually was. Staged deliverables in the same chat keep the context tight.

All 7 science sensors + all avionics + dual Teensys + LR1121 + GPS on one 110 mm disc is aggressive but achievable with 4-layer.

What changes with Board 1 removed
Physical stack (new):
Board A = old Board 2 (CDH + Sensors + Payload) — receives already-regulated +3V3 and +5V from off-board bucks via 2-pin JST-XH connectors
Board B = old Board 3 (Actuation) — receives +5V and +8V4 via the board-to-board header from Board A, OR directly from off-board bucks
Key decisions I need to make for you:
Where does +8V4_SERVO come from? You said "2 modules" — 3.3V buck and 5V buck. But the AGFRC A20BHM brake servos need 8.4V. Options:
(a) Add a third off-board buck (adjustable, set to 8.4V) also on the carbon fiber rod → clean, same philosophy
(b) Run AGFRC servos directly from battery (~11.1V nominal) → BAD, exceeds their 8.4V rating, will burn out
(c) Drop AGFRC, use only 5V servos → changes your whole descent mechanism
Recommended: (a) — add a third off-board buck for 8.4V. Same wiring style as the other two.
Battery INA226 — where does it live now? Options:
(a) On a tiny standalone breakout board inline with the battery wire, next to the bucks on the rod
(b) Move it onto Board A (CDH board), route battery+ up to it via a wire
(c) Skip it entirely, monitor only servo-side current on Board B
Recommended: (b) — put it on Board A. You need battery voltage/current visibility in your telemetry, and a separate breakout board on the rod is fragile.
Board-to-board header pinout — now carries: +5V, +8V4, +3V3 (all from off-board bucks arriving at Board A via connectors, then passed down/up to Board B), GND, plus the command bus. Cleaner and simpler than before.

Good news indeed — commercial-grade Ansys is a big upgrade from Abaqus/Explicit for your FEA work. That list is enormous (it's the full Ansys suite), and installing all of it would eat ~150-250 GB and take hours for nothing you'll use. Let me tell you exactly what to check.
What to select for CanSat work
3D Design
❌ Skip both Discovery installs (that's a separate simplified tool, not what you want for real FEA)
❌ Skip Geometry Service (PyAnsysGeometry) unless you're scripting geometry — not needed for CDR-level work
Structures (this is your core need — replaces Abaqus/Explicit)
✅ Mechanical Products — this is the main one. Static structural, explicit dynamics, transient — covers your opening-shock and terminal-glide FEA
✅ Autodyn — dedicated explicit solver, excellent for short-duration high-strain-rate events like your 0.1s deployment shock. Complements/can replace LS-DYNA for this
✅ Motion — if you want to simulate the parafoil deployment/brake-servo kinematics as a multibody mechanism, not just static FEA
❌ Skip LS-DYNA unless your license specifically includes it (often a separate paid module even under student/commercial licenses — check your license entitlement first)
❌ Skip Sherlock, Material Designer, Material Calibration App (used for PCB reliability & advanced material characterization — not needed at your stage)
❌ Skip RBF Morph, Customization Files, Sound-SAS under Structures
Fluids (only if you want to CFD-simulate parachute/parafoil descent aerodynamics)
✅ Fluent (includes CFD-Post) — if you want actual drag coefficient / descent rate validation via CFD instead of just hand calculations. This directly matters given your flagged descent-rate issue (14.4 m/s vs 15 m/s floor)
✅ CFD-Post only — already included with Fluent, don't need separately
❌ Skip everything else in Fluids (CFX, Polyflow, Chemkin, Rocky, ICEM CFD, TurboGrid, BladeModeler, FENSAP-ICE, Thermal Desktop, Forte, Reaction Workbench) — none relevant to your mission
Geometry Interfaces — select only the one matching your CAD tool
If you model in SolidWorks → ✅ SOLIDWORKS Geometry Interface
If Fusion 360/other → check which interface applies (Fusion isn't in this Ansys list directly — you may need to export as STEP/Parasolid instead)
✅ Parasolid Geometry Interface is a safe universal choice if unsure — most CAD tools can export Parasolid or STEP
Platform
✅ Dynamic Reporting — useful for generating clean report output for your CDR/PDR documentation
❌ Skip Remote Solve Manager (only needed for distributed/cluster computing, irrelevant on a single laptop)
Skip entirely (not relevant to CanSat):
Acoustics, Additive, Ansys AI Tools, Autonomous Vehicle Simulation, Connect, Digital Twin, Electronics (Electromagnetics Suite — unless you want to simulate your LR1121 antenna pattern, which is genuinely possible but advanced; skip for now), Embedded Software, Materials (Granta), Optics, Safety and Cybersecurity, remaining Geometry Interfaces (CATIA, NX, Inventor, Revit, etc. unless you specifically use those)
Minimal recommended selection
Structures:
  ✅ Mechanical Products
  ✅ Autodyn
  ✅ Motion (optional, for deployment kinematics)
Fluids:
  ✅ Fluent (includes CFD-Post)
Geometry Interfaces:
  ✅ [Your CAD tool's interface] or Parasolid
Platform:
  ✅ Dynamic Reporting
This gets you: static structural, explicit dynamics (shock), CFD (descent aerodynamics), and clean reporting — everything your PDR's FEA work needs, at a fraction of the install size and time.
One thing to verify before you finalize the install: check your student/commercial license entitlement file (or Ansys Licensing portal) to confirm which of these modules are actually authorized under your license — selecting a product you're not licensed for just wastes install space since it won't run anyway.

Select option 1 — "Can add a USB GNSS dongle (Recommended)."
Here's why this fits your setup: your L89 GPS is only onboard the CanSat. Right now the ground station has no independent position reference, so there's nothing to compare the CanSat's fix against — that's exactly what DGNSS needs. A cheap USB GNSS dongle (~₹800–1500, any u-blox NEO-6M/7/8 or similar with NMEA output) plugged into your ground station laptop gives you that second independent reference point.
How it would work in your system
Ground station dongle gets a fix at a known, surveyed position (your laptop just sits still during the flight).
Since the dongle knows its true position (after self-survey) but reports raw GPS coordinates with the same atmospheric/ionospheric error as any other receiver in the area, the software computes: error_vector = true_position − dongle_reported_position.
That error vector is applied as a correction to the CanSat's L89 GPS fix, coming in over your LR1121 telemetry link.
Result: 5–10 m raw GPS accuracy drops to ~1–2 m corrected accuracy — meaningfully better for your recovery walk.
Why not the other options for you
Option 2 (software-only) — no hardware cost, but caps you at raw single-receiver accuracy (5-10m). Fine as a fallback, but you asked specifically about adding this ground-station feature, so there's no reason to skip the hardware gain.
Option 3 — doesn't apply, you don't have an existing GS GNSS receiver yet.
One thing to flag before you commit
This DGNSS scheme only works if both receivers are close enough (same ionospheric conditions — a few km apart is fine, which your competition site easily satisfies) and the ground dongle gets a static, high-quality fix before/during flight for the self-survey step. Also confirm the receiving software you're building actually implements the correction math (differential correction isn't automatic — someone has to code the error-vector subtraction). If that's a separate build task, tell me and I can help draft that logic once you've picked the dongle.
Precise positioning needs fix-quality data the current 79-field schema doesn't carry (HDOP, fix type, geoid separation). New fields APPEND safely to the end of the schema — existing columns don't shift. Can the Teensy firmware be updated to send them?
Local DGNSS is the big accuracy win (5–10 m → 1–2 m) but it needs a GNSS receiver at the ground station so the software can measure the common-mode error. What's your situation?
answer both the qusetion for me
Question 1: Can the Teensy firmware send HDOP, fix type, and geoid separation?
Yes, straightforwardly. Here's why this is safe and easy:
Your L89 GPS module already outputs these fields in its raw NMEA sentences — you don't need new hardware, just to parse and forward data the module is already giving you:
HDOP — in the GGA sentence (field 8) and GSA sentence (field 16)
Fix type — in GGA (field 6: 0=no fix, 1=GPS fix, 2=DGPS fix) or GSA (field 2: 1=no fix, 2=2D, 3=3D)
Geoid separation — in GGA (field 11, the difference between WGS84 ellipsoid and MSL geoid)
Since you're appending to the end of the 79-field schema, this is non-breaking:
Your existing GCS parser code (which you built to tolerate variable field counts) will keep working with old logs
No existing column shifts, no re-validation of the first 79 fields needed
Just add 3 more fields to your Teensy's telemetry packing function and 3 more parser fields on the GCS side
Practical firmware change: wherever your Teensy currently parses the L89's UART NMEA stream (likely using TinyGPS++ or similar), you already have access to gps.hdop.value(), and you'll need to also parse the fix-type field from GSA (TinyGPS++ doesn't expose this natively — you may need a raw NMEA sentence tap or a library extension) and geoid separation from GGA (also often not exposed by TinyGPS++ directly — same caveat).
One flag: if you're using TinyGPS++, check whether it surfaces fix-type and geoid separation — many teams end up writing a small custom NMEA parser just for these two fields since TinyGPS++ focuses on lat/lon/alt/HDOP by default. Tell me your GPS parsing library and I can give you the exact field-extraction code.
Question 2: DGNSS ground station situation
Select "Can add a USB GNSS dongle (Recommended)."
Same reasoning as before — your ground station currently has no independent GNSS reference to compute the common-mode error against. A ~₹800–1500 u-blox USB dongle at the ground station gives you that second fix. The software self-surveys the dongle's static position, computes the live error vector against its own known-good coordinates, and applies that correction to the CanSat's incoming L89 fix — turning 5–10 m raw accuracy into 1–2 m corrected accuracy for your recovery walk.
This pairs naturally with Question 1: once your Teensy is sending fix-type and HDOP, your ground software can also use fix quality to weight or gate when DGNSS correction is trusted (e.g., don't apply correction confidently if the CanSat's fix type shows no 3D fix yet).

Yes, that's completely fine. Skip the DGNSS dongle.
What to select
Option 2 — "No GS receiver — software only."
What this means for you
Your L89 GPS keeps working exactly as it does now — raw GPS accuracy, typically 5–10 meters.
No extra hardware to buy, wire, or debug.
The ground software still gives you honest uncertainty circles (showing that 5-10m range on the map), multi-format coordinates, live walk-to-recovery navigation, and landing-point projection — you just don't get the extra correction step that would tighten it to 1-2m.
Is 5-10m actually a problem for you?
Realistically, no — for a CanSat recovery walk in an open field at Kushinagar, 5-10 meters is walkable in under a minute once you're in the general area. DGNSS matters a lot more for applications like precision agriculture or survey work, not for "walk to where the beeping/GPS marker says the CanSat landed."
About the HDOP/fix-type/geoid fields from your last question
Those are still worth adding regardless of your DGNSS decision — they don't depend on having a ground dongle. They just tell you how confident to be in whatever fix you're getting (e.g., "fix type = 3D, HDOP = 1.2" means trust it; "fix type = 2D, HDOP = 4.5" means the number could be off by more). That's useful even with a single-receiver, no-DGNSS setup, since it lets your GCS show an honest uncertainty circle instead of pretending every fix is equally good.
So: skip the dongle, but still add those 3 fields to your Teensy firmware if you want the uncertainty display to be meaningful rather than just showing a fixed generic circle.

Servo research: MG996R vs MG995
Spec	MG995	MG996R
Stall torque	9.4 kg·cm at 4.8V, 
Zbotic
 13 kg·cm at 6V (various sources)	Max stall torque 11 kg/cm at 6V 
Zbotic
Bearings	Single bushing on output shaft	Dual ball bearings supporting the output shaft 
Servo Database
 — much less play under lateral/side load

For your brake-line/parachute release mechanism: the dual ball bearing on MG996R matters — a brake-line servo takes lateral line tension, not just rotational load, and a bushing (MG995) wears faster under that side-load. Recommendation for your final design: MG996R over MG995 if choosing between these two, though your actual PDR-proposed AGFRC A20BHM (11.4 kg·cm @ 8.4V, 1.9× FEA margin) is still the superior choice for flight — these two are for bench characterization only, as you said.

Integration test — same data streamed to Serial Monitor AND your GCS in the exact 79-field CSV format
Order: LoRa (done) → Servos (MG996R + MG995) → BMP585 → ICM-20948 → L89 GPS → DS3231 RTC → TB6612FNG+motor

Project recall — VRITRA Team 025 (2026-INSPACe-CAN-7USAT-025)
Mission: Dual-stage descent CanSat — drag parachute + GPS-guided ram-air parafoil deploying at ~600m. Teensy 4.1 primary + backup flight computers, LR1121 LoRa telemetry, biochemical bacterial payload. Competition site: Kushinagar, UP.
Where we are:
Comms (LoRa): Fully debugged and range-tested. Found and fixed two real bugs — RadioLib's setOutputPower() was routing through the wrong PA pin (LP instead of HP) causing ~30dB loss, and explicit-header mode was letting corrupted noise through as "valid" packets at low SNR. Final fix: implicit header + HP PA forced + RxBoost on. Confirmed clean link at bench range (0% PER, RSSI -26 to -70dBm). Walked to ~418m before antenna orientation issues capped range — diagnosed as polarization/height/noise-floor problems, not firmware. Frequency plan locked: Team 025 = 867.00 MHz main / 865.75 backup, SF10, BW125, CR4/5, sync 0x98.
Hardware/BOM: ~₹27,176 spent of ₹100,000 cap. Confirmed received: Teensy 4.1 ×2, BMP585 ×3, ICM-20948 ×2, L89 GPS ×2, AP64500/AP62301 bucks ×3 each, TB6612FNG ×2, Core1121-HF (LR1121) ×3, DS3231, microSD, multimeter. BMS was removed from design (3S Molicel direct to bucks).
PCB architecture (locked, v3): 3-board stack, 110mm diameter, KiCad 10.0.5, JLCPCB 4-layer ENIG.
Board 1 (Sensors): all sensors + TCA9548A mux + AMS1117-1.8 LDO
Board 2 (Central Hub): battery + 3 bucks + INA226 + dual Teensy 4.1 + LR1121 + GPS + RTC + flash + camera + audio
Board 3 (Servos): Teensy 4.0 + TB6612FNG + 6 servo headers + INA226
Full click-by-click KiCad guide delivered (Phases 0-6), BOM v3 locked, PCB layout coordinates specified. Ansys 2026 R2 install guidance given (Mechanical + Autodyn + Fluent selected for FEA/CFD).
GCS: You shared cansat_gcs.py — PyQt5 desktop app, 79-field positional CSV schema (TEAM_ID,TIME_S,PKT,ALT_M,...), tolerant parser (missing trailing fields = absent, not zero), auto-reconnecting serial link, built-in simulator. I've mapped this schema exactly for component tests.
Component testing (current phase): Started per-component test protocol — baseline characterization + Serial/GCS integration test for each ordered part, in preparation for CDR. Just delivered Test 01: MG996R + MG995 servo bench test (parachute mechanism stand-in before final AGFRC A20BHM arrives) — includes both a raw sweep-timing test and a GCS-integrated version streaming to SRV1_DEG/SRV2_DEG.
Now: Teensy 4.1 + LoRa — Component Test 00

Two files, both carry every fix we found during range testing:
Transmitter.ino — flight-side, unchanged config from what worked at bench range (HP PA forced, implicit header, 867.00/865.75 MHz, SF10/BW125/CR4-5/sync 0x98 — all locked by the frequency plan). Beacons every 3s, or type text + Enter to send on demand.
Receiver_GCS_Integrated.ino — ground-side, new for this test cycle. Two things happen per received packet:

Serial Monitor on the receiver shows both the raw CSV line and the human-readable summary underneath it — good for confirming the GCS is parsing what you expect.
